EMPYREAN

An additive ambient synthesizer: twenty thousand partials per voice, on the graphics card

Version 0.25.0 · 356 parameters · 16384 presets · manual built 16.10.2026

The instrument

An additive ambient synthesizer: every voice is twenty thousand sine waves, computed on the graphics card. Each harmonic of a note is not one sine but a cloud of slightly detuned ones, every partial with its own slow drift, its own shimmer and its own place in the stereo field.

It plays by itself or under your hands. The conductor (Autoplay) is Noctuary's: a chord in just intonation that changes one voice at a time, minutes apart, in the spirit of Robert Rich's sleep concerts. Press Play in the header (or Space in the standalone) to start or stop it; play the keyboard over it, or switch it off and play alone. The standalone's window can be maximised from its title bar or fill the screen with F11 (Esc leaves); the pages grow with it.

The picture under the header shows the partials themselves, as the graphics card has them this moment: every eighth partial of every voice a point, its frequency across, its loudness up, leaving a trail that fades -- so you see each harmonic's cloud, and every partial rising, falling and wandering as the motions, the field and the landscapes move it. Green for what you play, violet for the conductor, pale blue for the second conductor, pale grey for the ghosts of the memory. Under it the keys, from A0 to C8, each where its pitch lies on the same axis: the notes that sound light up in the same colours (dimmer the further away and the softer they are), a small amber mark stands over the conductor's root, and the sounding notes' names are written in the picture's corner, low to high. On the right, the landscape the Aurora motion reads, with a line where the voices are in its loop.

The picture shows what the page in front edits, where it acts, and it can be taken by the hand. On Cloud & Filter, the filter's curve over the partials and the comb's notches where they stand: drag the curve's point across for the cutoff and up or down for the resonance; and the air's band (drag its centre and its edge) and the place where the brightness lets the harmonics fade (drag it). On Conductor, the conductor's register as a bracket over the keys: drag its ends. On Motion, the ripple's string, as the partials ride it. On Hall, the hall's memory and its lowest mode (drag it).

Canvas (the button in the picture's corner, or a double-click on it) gives the window to the picture alone: the partials large, the living landscape or the landscape faintly behind them, the keys, and the four macros at the bottom -- for listening, for a sleep concert on a screen. A click in the spectrum plays the key under it, louder the higher you click; drag to move from key to key. A double-click or Esc brings the pages back.

The header: the preset (its name opens the browser, the arrows step through the bank, A | B held lets you hear the preset as it was while your sound has moved from it), Autoplay's mode and Step -- a thin line under Step runs down to the conductor's next exchange -- and the scale, root and motion; in the second row what sounds, the output's meter (red at its end once it reached full scale; a click puts it out), and the tools: undo and redo, the search, the canvas, the night, the MIDI marks (every knob's learned controller shown on it), save, help and the settings (with the window's size and the zoom, which makes the controls themselves larger).

Finding your way. Ctrl+K (or the search in the header) opens the command palette: type a few letters -- they may be scattered, "mhd" finds Modal Hall Decay -- and any knob, preset or action is there; a knob's page opens and the knob lights up, and "cutoff 800" sets it. Words for how a sound sounds find presets by their measured traits: "dark calm wide", "bright moving", "pure narrow" (and dunkel, hell, ruhig, bewegt, breit, schmal, rein, luftig). Along the bottom of the window the info line tells what the control under the mouse does, its value now and what in the matrix moves it. On the first start a short tour shows the way in; Settings brings it back.

Night is a dark room's mode (the crescent in the header, the settings, the palette): the window dims and turns warm, the picture moves half as often, and a canvas left alone for a minute and a half fades further, until the mouse moves.

The pages stand in a strip under the picture, in sections: Focus; Sound (Cloud & Filter, Motion); Harmony (Tuning, Conductor); Space (Space, Effects, Hall); Modulation (Modulation, Matrix); Explore (Journey, Map). Beside each page's name a dot for every group with main controls tells what is on there -- lit in the group's colour while it plays, a faint ring while it rests -- and a page whose groups all rest has its name dimmed; Matrix counts the rows in use. Point at a page to read which groups are on and which are off.

Along the bottom, the modulation dock: every source a chip with its last four seconds drawn small -- the LFOs, envelopes and macros, the mod wheel, pressure, velocity and the random curve, then every other source a row of the matrix reads, and one more chosen with "+". Drag a chip onto any knob of any page and the source modulates it: a new row of the matrix at a fifth of the knob's range, its arc round the knob at once. Click a chip and the inspector shows the source: its trace, its own knobs (an LFO's shape and rate, a macro, the attractors' period ...) and every knob it moves, each with its depth and a button that removes the row; click a target's name to see its knob. Hold Alt and drag any knob: the source the inspector shows modulates it by as much as you drag (up more, down less or the other way). Every change to the matrix -- a chip dropped, a depth, a row removed -- is a step Ctrl+Z takes back. In a tall window the inspector stays docked over the dock; in a smaller one it opens over the pages' bottom and shuts with its x, the Inspector button or Esc.

The window opens as large as the screen allows it -- three quarters of the screen's width, at least 1280 x 860 -- and comes back where it was left -- the standalone's window at its place and size (maximised, if it was), on the page it was left on. Every page fits it without scrolling at 1280 x 860 and more: the boxes fill the rows as tightly as they can, the Focus page stands in two columns, and the picture gives way where a page needs the room; in a larger window the picture grows. A smaller window scrolls.

The pages are groups of knobs. A group's main controls -- what wakes it: the echo's level, the hall's, the sync's depth -- have their name in the group's colour and a soft halo; while they all stand at nothing (and nothing in the matrix moves them), the group rests: its other knobs dim, and "off" stands by its title.

Focus: the simple ways in

The Focus page is the way in without knowing the knobs. Every knob that shapes the sound was measured: around 48 presets it was moved a little and the sound measured again -- how much brighter, livelier, wider and noisier it became, and how much louder. What follows from that is here.

Nudge: Darker or Brighter, Narrower or Wider, Purer or Airier move the few knobs (up to four) that do the most for that measure and little for the others, the shortest way there, the level held where they leave room: brighter does not also mean louder or wider. A knob at nothing stays there -- a nudge never switches a layer on (the air, the melt, the hall). Calmer or Livelier go by a rule instead: every rate that moves something 1.23 times slower or faster, drift and shimmer a little less or more -- motion measured over seconds swings too much with slow cycles to steer by. Undo takes a press back.

Focus: the few knobs that do the most for this sound (those that are heard; at most three from one group), all the rest left on the pages.

Variations: six sounds near this one, a few heard knobs moved at random (none that is at nothing), more on the knobs that do more, the level held by the master as measured. They are there whenever the page is: made anew each time the sound has moved (not while the map or the morph plays it), each tile telling what it changes. Hold a tile to listen to it (the sound glides there and back when you let go); once heard, it offers Keep, which makes it the sound, and the next six are made around it -- so the sound evolves by your ear alone, a step at a time. New six draws others, Back returns to the sound before; the slider sets how far they go.

Teach: a macro learns a gesture by example. Press Teach (the sound now is the macro's place at its value now; a ring pulses round it while it learns), change any knobs, move the macro to where the change should be (1, say), and press Set here: the knobs go back, and the macro carries the change -- at 0 the sound as it was, at 1 the sound you made, between them half way. Cancel ends it with nothing learned. Up to four places; the small x beside a taught macro forgets. A lesson is a change, not a sound: it carries over to the next preset ("this much brighter and slower"), adds to the matrix's rows on the same macro, and is kept with the session.

Snapshots keep the sound as it is now -- every knob and the matrix, with a small picture of its partials: the first free tile is a "+" that takes one. Click one to come back to it (the sound glides there); right-click to put it into the morph's slot A or B (the slots' Preset ... menu on the Journey page lists them too), or to forget it. Up to eight; they are kept with the session.

The macros are large knobs here, named by what they move most: a lesson's furthest knob, or the target of their strongest matrix row.

Find: open or drop a recording (WAV or FLAC). Its brightness, motion, width and noisiness are measured as the presets' were, and the presets nearest it are listed -- click one to play it -- and ringed on the map.

The cloud

Spectrum shapes the harmonics: Tilt makes the upper ones fall away, Brightness closes a window over them, Odd / Even thins one kind, Inharmonic stretches them as a stiff string does. Stretch spreads every octave of the harmonics to that many cents (1200: harmonic): a stretched spectrum with the Timbre scale, which is made from the spectrum, gives a stretched tuning in which it sounds consonant again -- a harmony from another world. Shift moves every partial by the same number of hertz, as a frequency shifter does: the harmonics stop being multiples of the fundamental, and a chord turns metallic, bell-like, beating. The voice keeps its level whatever the shape: these colour the sound, they do not make it louder.

Cloud sets how wide each harmonic's cloud is (in cents, and how it widens towards the top), how far every partial drifts in pitch and how far its level shimmers, and how far the partials scatter across the stereo field. Purity (Tuning) narrows the clouds too, so that just intervals can lock.

Air adds a band of noise made of partials around a multiple of the fundamental: Centre, Width and Flicker (how fast its partials quiver -- which is what makes them heard as noise).

Turbulence is the bloom of a struck gong or tam-tam. In a real plate the modes are not alone: struck hard, they hand their energy on to the modes above them, the more the louder they are. Here every harmonic of a note does that -- seven tenths of what it passes on to the next harmonic, three tenths to its octave -- by the cube of how hard the note was played and how loud the instrument sounds, and Settle is how long the spectrum takes to come back to its own shape when it grows soft. The power moves, it is not made: a soft chord stays pure, a loud dense swell breaks slowly into a shimmering glow above itself without growing louder.

Motion and landscapes

Every partial has envelopes of its own, on three lanes: loudness, pitch and place. A motion sets them all at once: Random (every partial for itself, a fine glitter), Wave (a wave climbing through the spectrum), Pendulum (each harmonic pulses a little faster than the one below, over a bass that stays), Breathe (loudness, place and pitch travel through each cloud), Glissando (every partial glides into its note from up to fifteen semitones away), Aurora (the partials read a landscape).

Your own landscape: click the landscape beside the picture and open a picture (PNG, JPEG, BMP, TIFF, GIF) or a recording (WAV, FLAC). A picture is read with time from left to right (a minute at Rate 1) and the frequencies from the bottom (20 Hz) to the top (20 kHz), its brightness as loudness; a recording brings its own motion, the colour taken out of it. It becomes the landscape Your Own, and if nothing reads a landscape yet the motion becomes Aurora. The file is remembered with the project.

A landscape is time against frequency: every partial reads the column at its own frequency. Aurora, Shepard (bands climbing for ever), Tides, Rain (short glints) and Choir (the formants of u-o-a-e-i, the vowel turning slowly). Rate and Depth scale whatever the motion does; Depth 0 leaves the bare cloud.

The field moves the partials as a whole, on top of any motion. Scale pulls every partial towards the nearest pitch of the scale: a wide or stretched cloud gathers into the key and keeps only a narrow shimmer. Swarm lets a few attractors (Attractors) wander through the spectrum on chaotic paths (Wander is how fast) and gathers the partials within their reach (Radius); Scatter pushes the partials away from them instead, gaps moving through the sound like dust parted by a wind. Flock makes every cloud a flock of starlings: each partial keeps to its neighbours' place and pace without coming closer to them than half the distance they keep at rest, and a wind of its own blows each cloud about, so the partials swirl, bunch and spread around their harmonic. Pull is how fast the partials may move, Spring how strongly they are drawn back to their own places. The partials keep their own phases while they gather, so the level stays.

Orbit makes every partial a charged particle in pitch and side. The scale's pitches are wells it is drawn into, and a magnetic field bends every move it makes, so instead of settling it circles: fast small circles riding a slow circling of the well, in pitch and across the field at once -- a chorus that turns. Pull is how deep the wells are, Wander how strong the field (how fast they circle), Spring the damping (light, the circles keep their course; heavy, they wander), Radius the cents to a full side, and the orbits about half of it. Orbits wider than the scale's steps hop from pitch to pitch.

Physarum makes a share of the partials (Crawlers) crawl as a slime mould does: each senses a trail ahead, ahead left and ahead right, turns towards the most, moves and leaves trail; the trail spreads and fades (Trail), and the notes' harmonics feed it. Veins grow between the harmonics and partials glide along them from one to the next; as the chord changes, the network moves to the new food and the old veins starve (Jones, after Physarum polycephalum). Pull is how fast they crawl, Radius how far ahead they sense -- short, they keep to the harmonics; long, they wander the veins --, Spring how they are drawn back home.

The living landscape is a field that lives on the graphics card while you listen, and every partial reads it at its frequency (across) and its place in its cloud (along), so each harmonic is a slice through the living pattern. Lenia is Conway's Life made continuous: creatures that drift, pulse, split and die. Turing is Gray-Scott reaction-diffusion, the chemistry behind a leopard's spots: Character leads it from spots through dividing cells, coral and worms to mazes (for Lenia it sets the growth). Life Rate is how fast it lives, Life Depth how far the partials follow it, Zoom how much of its width the spectrum spans. A field that dies or fills up is seeded again; the level is kept from the field's own measure. While it lives, the panel beside the picture shows it.

The living landscape can also be an animated picture, made with the continuous transforms of the Kaleidoscope visualizer: Kaleidoscope folds the landscape (yours too) into turning mirror wedges round a slowly wandering centre; Tunnel flies down a tunnel lined with it, rings born in the middle and running out to the low and the high; Spiral winds it into a spiral that zooms for ever -- its patterns travel through the spectrum without end; Flow carries it on a current of noise like ink in water; Feedback feeds the field back into itself, turned and zoomed, so it spirals outward and never repeats; Rain lets drops fall on a pond, their rings spreading and crossing; Starfield streams stars out of the middle; Moire lays two turning gratings over each other, their broad bands wandering slowly. Life Rate is how fast it flows, Turn how fast it turns (turns a minute), Sides the mirror wedges, spiral arms or lines, Character how strongly it distorts. What moves across the picture is heard as the colour wandering; what moves along it, within each harmonic's cloud, as its shimmer and width.

Two more fields answer to what is played. Fluid is a real fluid (a lattice Boltzmann simulation) in which every note blows ink and momentum at its own frequency and at its octave: the plumes rise, roll up into eddies, shed vortices and drift into the frequencies around them over minutes, and where the ink is, the partials sound. Character goes from syrup to turbulence, Life Rate is how fast it flows; the ink fades over about twenty-five seconds. Islands is the separation of oil and water (Cahn-Hilliard): a fine-grained field gathers on its own into ever fewer, larger regions that drift slowly across the spectrum -- an unsettled sound calming over ten minutes into a few broad islands of colour. Character goes from a fine labyrinth to a few large islands, Life Rate is how fast it gathers.

Partial Sync gives every partial a slow oscillator of its own, which moves its loudness (Sync), at a rate scattered around Sync Rate by Spread. Coupling pulls the members of each harmonic towards their common phase -- the mathematics of fireflies falling into step. Loosely coupled, each partial flickers for itself and the flickering averages away; past a critical strength they lock, and the whole cloud of a harmonic pulses as one. Turn Coupling slowly and you hear a crowd find its rhythm.

Sync Mode chooses how they couple. Global is the above. Chimera couples each partial only to its neighbours round its cloud, with a little lag (Lag): then an ordered part and a disordered part live side by side -- a clear pulsing core in a flickering mist -- and the border between them wanders. Swarm makes every partial a swarmalator: it also has a place on a small circle in pitch and in the field (Reach is the circle's size in cents, and with it how far it moves from side to side), and place and phase pull each other (Swarm: positive, like phases gather; negative, they part). Their states are heard clearly: with Coupling at nothing and Swarm up, or Coupling up and Swarm below nothing, a phase wave -- every place its own phase, so the loudest part of the cloud circles through pitch and field at Sync Rate, a slow vortex; with both up, two groups opposite each other on the circle, low on one side and high on the other, pulsing in turn; between these, the wave broken into pieces that pulse against each other; with Swarm well below nothing, each partial for itself.

Swarmalators let the partials ride a swarm round you: a thousand oscillators that swarm in the plane while they synchronise, place and phase pulling each other (O'Keeffe, Ha and Strogatz). Every partial takes its place left to right and near to far from one of them, and its loudness pulses with its phase. Like Gathers: like phases come together; Fall In Step: near ones pulse together (or apart). Their states: everything gathered and pulsing as one; a still disc of phases; a ring whose phase turns round it, so the pulse circles you; that ring broken into turning clusters (Swarm Spread); and, as Fall In Step goes below nothing, the ring itself turning. Swarm Speed: how fast it settles and moves; Swarm Pulse: the pulse's rate.

Dispersion makes each partial's clock late by its pitch. Positive, the low ones run up to that many seconds late: an attack or a glint falls from the top as a cascade, like a drop on ice. Negative, the high ones are late and it rises. On a held sound it is heard where the sound changes: attacks, releases, the glints of the landscapes.

Time Bend gives every partial a clock of its own that runs slower where the spectrum is dense -- as time does near a heavy mass: its drift, its shimmer and its sync's pulse slow down where much sounds, and run on at their pace where little does. Below nothing it is the other way round: the crowded bands quicken and flicker, the sparse ones stay calm. Turning it never jumps.

The harmonics' own lives

Every note's harmonics can live lives of their own here: registers that overblow, families that take turns, avalanches that run up them, clouds pumped below them -- and the horizon they crowd under, and the grains the partials may sound in.

Overblow gives every note a breath its registers compete for, as in a flute: with little breath the fundamental sounds; with more the octave takes over -- the odd harmonics fade and the note sounds an octave up -- then the twelfth and the double octave. The register that sounds holds on, so it jumps up later than it falls back, and a note near the edge flickers between two. The breath is the knob, by how hard the note was played, and with MPE its pressure (Press: Breath); Overblow Time is how long a register takes to take over.

Subharmonics goes the other way from Turbulence. A loud harmonic pumps a cloud at half its frequency -- the fundamental one an octave below, the third harmonic one a fifth above the fundamental -- and that cloud in turn one at half again: the period doubles, and doubles again, as in a Faraday experiment or a parametric amplifier. It begins from nothing and grows slowly near its threshold, faster above it: hold a loud chord, and after a while a dark floor opens beneath it; let it go, and the floor closes again. The power comes from the pumping harmonic, so the voice grows no louder. The pump is the knob, how hard the note was played, its envelope and MPE's Breath; Sub Time is how fast the clouds grow and fade. A note keeps its subharmonic clouds if it starts while Subharmonics is above nothing.

Horizon bends the harmonics towards a horizon they never pass, Horizon Height octaves over each note: the lowest stay where they were, the higher ones crowd ever closer below it, hundreds of them within the last few hertz -- a bright halo over a plain bass. Half way, the harmonics are bent half way; moved slowly, the halo travels over the note like a curtain. The conductors, Self-Tune and the Timbre scale hear the bent harmonics too.

Seasons lets five families of harmonics take turns: the octaves of the fundamental (2, 4, 8 ...), of the fifth (3, 6, 12 ...), of the third (5, 10 ...), of the seventh (7, 14 ...) and of the ninth (9, 18 ...). Each beats the next two and loses to the two before, as in rock-paper-scissors: one family swells, the one that beats it grows on it and takes over, and so round -- a slow round of seasons that never settles and never dies out, the fundamental always under it. Year is the time of a whole round; every voice is in the same season.

Avalanche lays a rice pile on every note's harmonics (the Oslo model, from the physics of critical systems). Grains fall on it now and then (Grains Falling); where its slope grows too steep it slides, and a slide may set off the next, so avalanches run up and down the harmonics, each harmonic that slides lit for a moment (Glow). Most are small glints, some run through a dozen harmonics, and rarely one rolls through all of them for many seconds -- the statistics of sand piles and earthquakes, with no dice deciding when.

Grain lets a share of the partials sound in grains instead of on and on: each a short swell (Grain Length) for a part of its time (Grain Density), at a phase of its own, and every grain born at a new place in its cloud and in the stereo field. Few and short, the cloud glitters like rain on leaves or ice; long and dense, it breathes. A grain partial's power over time is what it had sounding on, so the level stays.

Talbot lets the first 24 harmonics of every note evolve as a wave function does, from the note's start. Revival: they fall apart into a fine pattern, the finest detail first, are both at once at a quarter, stand upside down half way (the energy at the top instead of the bottom) and come back exactly whole after the period, lingering a while at each of these -- Talbot's effect, from optics, on a clock that slows down at them (on a plain one each would last a few milliseconds). Bloch: the energy swings up the harmonics and back down once a period, as an electron does in a crystal under a field. Depth mixes it with the timbre at rest; the voice's power stays.

Ripple stretches a string along the spectrum. Every new note strikes it at its harmonics, and the ripples run up and down the spectrum (Ripple Speed, in octaves a second), reflect at its ends, cross each other and die away (Ripple Decay); every partial's loudness rides the wave where it stands (Ripple). A chord change sends a shiver through everything that sounds. Bend slows the wave where the notes sound, down to a fifth or less of its speed: the waves linger at the notes, pile up there and are partly thrown back, as light near a heavy mass.

Breathers makes the string a chain of pendulums (the sine-Gordon equation). A strike can no longer run away as a wave: it stays where it fell as a breather, a hump that pulses there, slower than once a second, and drifts slowly up or down; a longer Ripple Decay lets the breathers live longer.

Phantoms and dreams

Layers that are not quite notes, made for the night.

Phantom plays a melody that is in neither ear: noise the same in both ears, except that at a pitch the right ear's phase turns once round against the left. The brain hears a faint, clear tone there (Huggins' pitch) -- with headphones only; through loudspeakers it is the noise. Phantom Line: a slow melody of its own in the scale, the notes that just ended (Echo), or the chord. Phantom Width: how sharply the phase turns. The layer is heard dry, after everything, so the echoes and rooms do not blur it.

Dream: every three seconds the instrument keeps what it heard, if it is new; a dream wanders among these memories by association -- the nearest ones to where it is, blended by how alike they are, a little noise moving it on (a modern Hopfield network) -- and is sung far off by a cloud of its own. Over a night the harmonies of hours ago come back, blurred and linked to each other. Dream Focus: a blend, or one memory at a time; Dream Drift: how restless; Dream Depth: how far off.

Premonition: the conductor's notes are heard coming. Before a note begins, its cloud swells wide and far, as a reverb would backwards, and is drawn into the note when it starts. Only the conductor's notes: the instrument knows them before they sound, the keys it cannot.

Memory keeps a ghost of every note let go. It rises over Bloom once the note has gone, fades over Memory Time, and while it fades it widens, drifts and darkens (Blur) and recedes into the far plane (Sink): the harmony of the last minutes stands faintly behind the present one.

Dream Journal keeps the dream's memories -- the newest thousand, a byte a band -- with the project or the set: open it again and the dream goes on from what it remembered, nights later.

Streams parts each voice into streams and gives each a slow vibrato and tremolo of its own: partials that move together are heard as one sound, and partials that move apart as several (what the ear does with a room full of voices, Bregman's common fate). Streams: the odd harmonics and the even ones -- a hollow clarinet and an octave above it --, the harmonics by three, the registers, or every member of a cloud on its own (the cloud parts into lines). Stream Motion: how much they move (nothing: the feature is off); Stream Rate: how fast. Fusion turns it round: at nothing every stream moves its own way and the voice falls apart into its streams, at full they all move as one and it fuses again -- turn it slowly and one sound becomes two and becomes one.

Risset lets the voices pulse in Jean-Claude Risset's rhythm that slows for ever: five layers of a pulse an octave of tempo apart, all slowing by an octave in Octave Every, each weighted by where it stands among the octaves -- the slowest fading out below while a new fastest comes in above, every layer's beats on the slowest's -- so the pulse keeps slowing and never arrives, as Shepard's scale keeps falling. Risset Pulse: its tempo in the middle; Risset Way: slowing for ever, or quickening for ever.

Wind and weather

The field has a wind: a real flow (lattice Boltzmann) pushed across it, shedding vortex streets from three posts, the same for everything it moves. Wind: calm below a quarter, a breeze at half, a gale at the top; Gusts: steady air or squalls.

Chimes hangs 2048 tuned chimes over the field -- left to right, near to far -- in a wind: a real flow (lattice Boltzmann) pushed across the field, shedding vortex streets from three posts. Every chime's clapper is a pendulum the wind swings; where it strikes, the chime rings (each mode a doublet, so it beats as a real chime does). Gusts cross the field and the chimes ring in waves, from one side to the other. Chime Kind: metal tubes, glass, bamboo, small bells; they are tuned to the scale, in their kind's register moved by Chime Pitch.

Rain falls over the field where the showers are, and the wind carries the showers across (Showers: even rain, or showers passing). Drops: how many fall a second over the whole field. Falls On: on a pond every drop traps a bubble that sings for a few milliseconds -- Minnaert's resonance, higher the smaller the bubble, rising as it rises --; on leaves it ticks, on stone it clicks, on a tin roof it rings the sheet. Tuned Drops lets a share of the bubbles and of the roof's rings sing the scale's pitches.

Leaves rustle where the wind blows, harder where it gusts: four thousand clusters over the field, every leaf a click through its own small resonance, so a gust is heard sweeping across. Foliage: broad leaves, pine needles (a high hiss), tall grass, dry leaves (a sparse crackle).

Surf: a sea breaks on a shore along the field. Sixty-four trains of swell about its period (Swell) come in a little off straight, so crests run along the shore and come in sets; where a crest rises past its break it foams, and the foam hisses, fading over seconds. The wind raises the waves. Shore: sand hisses, pebbles crackle in the backwash, rocks boom as each wave breaks. Shore Distance: how far off it is.

The night

Sounds of the night outside, each computed as it happens rather than played back.

The Aeolian harp stands in the field's wind: twelve strings, tuned to the notes of the chord about 55 to 110 Hz (Harp Pitch moves them by octaves). A string in the wind sheds vortices -- at a rate that grows with the wind's speed over the string's thickness (Strouhal's number) -- and the vortices drive the string, and the string's motion drives them back (Facchinetti's wake oscillator): so the string locks onto the harmonic nearest its vortices and holds it, and jumps to the next when the wind has moved on. A harp sings its harmonics, changing as the wind gusts. Gauge: thin strings sing higher harmonics in the same wind, thick ones lower; the wind is the Weather page's.

Thunder: a storm whose strikes come at random, more often the higher Thunder is. Every strike lays out a lightning channel of its own -- from the cloud's base, one and a half to four kilometres up, down to the ground, tortuous at every scale and branched -- and every one of its four thousand segments sends you a pressure wave (an N-wave, as long as the channel's energy says): a segment across your line of sight claps, one along it rumbles. The waves come as late as they are far, three seconds a kilometre -- the crack of the near part first, the rolling of the rest for seconds after -- and the air takes the highs of the far ones. Storm Distance: how far off; Rumble: a single crack, or a long rolling rumble from a branched channel. The source Lightning flashes as a strike happens, before its thunder arrives.

Singing ice: a frozen lake under the cold, heard from its shore. The ice is a sheet of slider blocks: a part past its strength slips and hands its stress to its neighbours, which may slip in turn -- cracks of every size, the large ones rare, as in a real sheet (Olami, Feder and Christensen). Each crack is heard across the ice as a chirp falling from the highs: waves of bending in a plate run the faster the higher they are, so the highs arrive first and the pitch falls as they follow -- the laser-like song of lake ice. Ice Thickness: thin ice is slow and sings long, thick ice short and quick; Cold: how often it cracks.

The night chorus: two thousand insects and frogs over the field, eight kinds, each calling in a band of its own (the acoustic niche: a cricket's chirp at 4.6 kHz, a tree cricket's trill, a katydid's rasp high above, a peeper's rising peep, a tree frog's pulsed call, a bullfrog's deep drone). Every caller is a clock that calls when it comes round; a call of its own kind heard close enough resets it towards its start (Greenfield's resetting), so neighbours fall into step or into turns, and a caller about to call into a louder one of another kind in its band waits for a gap. Who Calls: a meadow, the treetops, a pond, or all; Warmth: warm insects call faster (Dolbear's law: the snowy tree cricket's chirps a minute are about seven times the degrees less thirty), and the night arc cools the night as it deepens; Answering: how far a call resets the neighbours; Tuned Calls: the carriers pulled to the scale.

Night Arc follows the real night where you are: the sun's height from the date, the time and the place (Settings > Night arc; at first the main city of your time zone), turned into a night that is 0 while the sun is up and 1 from astronomical night on, through the twilights. Night Arc darkens, slows, thins and widens the sound by it -- the brightness and the tilt, the conductor's rate and density, the far plane --; the source Night does whatever you route it to.

Magnetosphere lets the instrument listen as a radio does to the Earth's magnetic field (VLF, the way Voyager and the receivers in Antarctica hear it). Whistlers: a lightning stroke's click runs along a field line through the plasma and comes out drawn into a falling tone -- the higher frequencies first (t = D / sqrt(f)), from ten kilohertz down to a couple of hundred over seconds, with echoes after it from bouncing between the hemispheres; Whistlers a minute, and every strike of Thunder sends one. Dispersion: how far it is drawn out. Dawn Chorus: rising tones in bursts, like birds at dawn, each sweeping up faster the louder it is (Omura's nonlinear growth), in a band set by Gyrofrequency. Hiss: the plasmasphere's structureless hiss.

Space and gravity

Star lets a star be heard. A star rings in hundreds of modes at once, each a standing sound wave inside it -- what asteroseismology listens to with the Kepler and TESS telescopes, transposed here from millihertz into the audible. No clock drives them: the convection's noise keeps each one ringing, so its loudness wanders as a bell struck by a thousand small hands does, faster for the broad lines, slower for the narrow ones. Star Kind: a Sun -- its modes almost evenly spaced (the large separation, set by Star Pitch against the root), each order in four degrees with pairs of lines a little apart (the small separation, which narrows with Star Age as the core burns); a Red Giant -- fewer orders, every dipole mode broken into a cluster of narrow lines where the sound waves meet the core's gravity waves, crowding as it ages; a White Dwarf -- gravity modes evenly spaced in period, which is an undertone series, only some of them driven, their periods growing as it cools. Spin splits every mode into its azimuthal orders, a slow beating chorus, and spreads them across the field.

Black Hole puts one in the field, at Hole Place. A share of the partials falls into it: over Fall they sink from far out to the horizon, seen from far away -- each redshifted (its pitch times the square root of one less the horizon's radius over its distance), dimmed, drawn to the hole, and frozen at the horizon as it fades; then it is born again far out. The rest is lensed: what lies behind the hole is bent out into a ring round it and brightened, as a galaxy behind a cluster is.

Merger lets two black holes merge, now and then (at 1 one every three minutes on average). The wave they send rises for Inspiral seconds, from half a hertz -- too slow to hear: it stretches and squeezes the field's width instead, faster and faster -- until, at the last stable orbit, it is a tone in the bass that sweeps up into the ringdown: the hole that remains rings in its quasi-normal modes and dies within a few dozen cycles. Ringdown sets the pitch it rings at (the heavier, the lower); Mass Ratio the spin it is left with, and unequal masses add a second mode above. The source Merger follows the wave for the matrix.

Scintillation lets the partials twinkle as starlight does. A thin screen of turbulence (Kolmogorov's: whirls of every size) drifts across the line of sight, and every band hears it through its own Fresnel zone -- the phase the screen lends it growing with its wavelength, as a plasma's does --, so bright and dark patches wander over pitch and time, the low ones broad and slow, the high ones fine. Scattering: weak (a shimmer) to strong (deep patches). Drift: how fast it moves.

Spectral Lines: Lines draws every harmonic to a line of hydrogen's spectrum -- the h-th harmonic to the h-th line, from the Pfund series at the note up through Brackett, Paschen and Balmer to Lyman six octaves above, each series crowding to its limit. Heat is the gas's: each line's Doppler width (the same in cents at every line, for Doppler's width is a share of the frequency). Zeeman: a magnetic field splits every line into a triplet, slowly beating. Absorption carves hydrogen's dark lines, on the root, into everything that sounds -- best heard on a continuum: a wide cloud, the air, the rain --, as a star's light is seen through a cool gas.

Wreck: a derelict's structure -- a cylindrical Hull, a spherical Tank, a long Truss, a perforated Plate --, its modes worked out from a model of masses and springs (Tools/presets/make_wreck.py), its lowest tuned to the root (Wreck Size). Micrometeorites strike it (Hits a minute), each at a place of its own, waking the modes that move there; thermal snaps (Snaps) run over it in creaks of tiny slips -- six times as often at dusk and dawn when the night arc is on. Ring: how long its lowest modes ring.

Throat (on the Dreams page): the throat of the abyss -- vocal tracts as long as a giant's (Tract, 17 to 45 cm: the formants an octave and more down), modelled as a tube of many sections the sound runs back and forth in (Kelly and Lochbaum), each driven by a glottal pulse on the root, its fifth, its octave (Throats). Kargyraa: the ventricular folds close every other cycle, as in the Tuvan and Tibetan chant, and the voice sounds an octave below itself. Vowel: u o a e i, slowly drifting.

Redshift (on the Hall page): the modal hall's modes glide down for ever, each from a place of its own, and are born again at the top, three octaves up -- every tail sinks into the deep as it dies, and the hall never runs out of modes below. Redshift Time: an octave of the glide.

Filter and envelope

The filter is read as a frequency response at every partial: LP 6, LP 12, LP 24, HP 12, BP 12, Notch, Peak and Formant (whose cutoff picks the vowel). Env Amount moves the cutoff with the voice's envelope, in octaves; Key Track follows the note; Drift lets the cutoff wander on its own. MIDI controller 74 moves the cutoff.

The spectral comb cuts notches into the spectrum, evenly spaced per octave (Density), as deep as Comb says -- read at every partial, so it needs no delay line and can be anything from one notch an octave to twelve. Sweep moves the notches through the spectrum, in notches a second: since the comb repeats every notch, it travels on without end, a phaser that rises (or, negative, falls) forever. Stereo combs the left and the right of the field apart, so the notches travel crosswise through the space. The level is kept whatever the depth.

The envelope is every voice's: Attack, Decay, Sustain, Release, in seconds to minutes.

Tuning

Scale chooses the tuning: equal temperament, the just scales, the harmonic series, slendro, Bohlen-Pierce, or Timbre -- the scale the cloud's own spectrum asks for (Sethares). Root is its tonic; Keys maps the keyboard (snapped to twelve keys, or one key per degree). Purity blends equal temperament (0) with the scale (1). Tide leans the whole instrument by a few cents on a curve of minutes.

Self-Tune lets a chord tune itself. Where a harmonic of one voice nearly meets a harmonic of another (within Reach), the two voices pull together until they no longer beat: an equal-tempered major third settles to a pure one within seconds, and gives way again when the chord changes. Anchor draws every voice back towards its pitch in the scale.

Under the knobs, the octave over the root as a ruler: equal temperament's twelve steps as faint lines with their names, the scale's pitches as lines with how far each stands from its step in cents (+4, -14, -49 ... for the harmonics 8 to 16), and every voice as a light where it sounds -- higher up for a higher octave, green for yours, violet for the conductor's -- with a tail as long as Self-Tune pulls it towards a pure interval. Purity is said under it: how far the voices stand from equal temperament towards the scale. Click a line to hear its pitch, in the octave the height says, for as long as the button is down; Shift-click holds it (Shift-click it again to let it go), so two or three held pitches can be heard locking into a pure interval while Purity and Self-Tune move. Leaving the page lets every held note go.

The conductor

Free lets notes come and go on their own timers. Chords (Autoplay) keeps the chord full and exchanges one voice at a time, every so many seconds (Every) or now (Step): the new note is the one that sits best against the voices that stay, within Voice Lead semitones. Tension allows notes that lean; Root Move lets the harmony travel. Timbre judges consonance from the cloud's own spectrum.

Lowest - Highest is one bar with two ends, the register the conductor chooses from (the bracket over the keys in the picture is the same); Hold Min - Hold Max likewise, the span a Free note is held. The thin line under Step in the header runs down to the next exchange.

Foundation gives the music a floor: one voice holds the conductor's root in the octave from C2 up (from Lowest, when the register begins lower), as long as the root stands, and moves with it when the root moves; the chord is drawn from a fifth above it, so nothing crowds or muddies the bass. It stands close and follows the chord's loudness -- half a voice under a lone note, a little more than one voice under a full chord, so it neither drowns a chord still arriving nor vanishes under a full one; the knob sets its share. At 0 there is none and the conductor chooses over the whole register as before. Every preset in the bank has one (since 0.22.0).

The floor (Floor, on the Conductor page): the foundation keeps its depth -- whatever filter the sound goes through, a formant, a band pass, a low cut, its fundamental and its second harmonic come through it. Floor sets the share of the sound under 150 Hz it holds at the least: while rain, a harp or a bright chord cover it, it is raised, by up to 12 dB, slowly, and let down again as it comes through. Pedal holds its note while the conductor's root moves -- for up to three minutes, then it follows; at 1 it stays on the key's tonic for ever, an organ point under everything that moves above it. Pressure puts a share of its partials a beat above the rest (Pressure Rate at the fundamental, its harmonics beating as many times faster): the floor pulses slowly, as two organ pipes a hair apart do. Sub Octave adds a voice an octave below it, Fifth one a fifth above (the chord then begins an octave above the foundation); Breath lets the two swell and ebb, the sub octave in 26 seconds, the fifth in 42.

Follow Light lets the conductors choose where the landscape is bright. Every candidate note is weighed by how much of its harmonics fall into the light -- the living landscape's columns while it lives, otherwise the landscape the Aurora motion reads, as a new note would read it over its first seconds -- against the register's mean; at 1, a note in twice the light is eight times as likely. The harmony then comes where the light is, and the two halves of the instrument speak to each other.

Brain 2 is a second conductor in the background: its root follows the first one's plus Interval, it plays slowly (Rate 2), few notes (Density 2), deep in the space (Depth 2).

The quantum walk moves the conductor's root on the Tonnetz -- the pitch classes joined by fifths and by major and minor thirds -- not by dice but as a wave: twelve amplitudes that spread along the joins and interfere. Every Look Every seconds it is looked at, and the root moves to the pitch class where it is found, drawn by how much of the wave is there; the wave is then partly collapsed onto it and walks on, so it remembers where it might have gone. Spread: how far it spreads between two looks -- a little, and the root moves to a neighbour on the Tonnetz; much, and the interference decides, so that some roots come up again and again and others hardly ever.

Space

Every note stands on a plane between the ear (0) and the far background (1): the conductor puts some notes near and most deep (Depth), played notes stand at Keys Depth. A far note is quieter, darker and heard only through the far reverb. Breath lets every voice's plane wander; Doppler raises a voice that comes closer. Presence lifts the 2-5 kHz band on the near plane; Pad Low Cut leaves the bottom free.

The far reverb (Level, Size, Decay, Damping) is Noctuary's, with its Diffusion (the swell before the hall), Pre-Delay, Unmask (the hall steps aside, band by band, wherever the near plane sounds), its four networks (Far Mode: Colourless rings least, Rotating lets the tail drift) and Freeze.

Below the knobs the space is seen from above: you at the bottom, the near plane close to you, the far plane at the rim (glowing as loud as the far reverb). Every voice is a light at its plane, across from its pitch (low notes left, high right), as large as it is loud, in the colour of whose note it is; Breath lets it wander, and its trail shows where it was. Drag the dashed rings to set Depth (where the conductor puts its notes) and Keys Depth (where yours stand).

Binaural (for headphones) places every partial of the near plane at a point of its own round the head: the head's shadow, the folds of the ear and the time the sound takes round the head, worked out for each partial at its own frequency. Surround spreads the places from the front half to all round, Height lifts and lowers them, Orbit turns the whole cloud round the listener. The far plane stays as it was, heard through the far reverb; the master's width and mono bass step aside while Binaural is on. Master: the level, the bass made mono below Bass Mono, and the width.

Echoes and rooms

Echo is Noctuary's: two delays whose times glide and wander, a little different in each ear. Cross takes each side's feedback partly from the other, Damping darkens the loop, Absorb narrows it to a band as the feedback rises (so long echoes drown in fog instead of merely getting quieter), and Duck darkens it while the voices are loud, so a new note does not have to fight the last one's tail. To Far sends the echoes into the far plane, where they recede into the far reverb. Echo 2 runs after the first and hears its echoes: longer chains. The small chain between Time L and Time R links them: turning one turns the other in proportion.

Room gives the near plane a place to stand: the early reflections of a room (Room Size, Absorb), arriving from where the voices stand, and a small reverb (Near Reverb, Near Decay, Near Damping).

The spectral echo takes the sound apart into five hundred bands and gives each a delay line of its own. Tilt brings the high bands back later than the low ones (or earlier), Scatter gives every band a time of its own, Feedback and Damping set how long and how dark the repeats are, To Far how much of them recedes. An attack comes back as a cascade, a chord as a spreading shimmer.

The melt holds the sound's spectrum and lets it melt: two thousand bands, each keeping what it heard and fading over Melt Time, while the held spectrum slowly flows into its neighbours (Spread: how many octaves it has spread after a second). What has been held longest is spread widest, so a chord leaves a glow that widens into the space between its notes. A phase vocoder sings it again, each band at the frequency it heard, drawn towards its own pitch as it melts, a little apart in each ear. To Far sends it into the far plane.

Bloom: a large plate -- a tam-tam, a thunder sheet -- that everything in the field rings, the voices, the chimes and the weather. Its modes stand evenly in hertz, as a plate's do (Plate Size: small and sparse and bright, or a mode every hertz). A plate struck hard is not a hall: its energy climbs to the high modes, the shimmer that blooms out of a tam-tam a moment after the stroke, the faster the louder (Bloom Drive) -- the cascade of weak wave turbulence, the energy kept. Gong Glide: the pitch rises with the energy, the low modes most, as an opera gong's does (or falls). Plate Decay: how long it rings at 200 Hz.

The wave room and the halo

The wave room puts the voices into a room simulated as air: the wave equation on a grid of a quarter of a million cells, eleven centimetres apart, six thousand steps a second. Its modes, its standing waves and its flutter between parallel walls are not added: they happen, below about 1.5 kHz (above, the other rooms and halls do their part). Every voice sounds from where it stands, round in front of you and as far as its distance; your two ears are a cell either side of you. Room Size: from a chapel to a hall of ten by eight metres and four and a half high; Walls: stone that rings, or curtains that take it up; Listener: where you stand, from the front to the back -- walk, and the bass swells and thins as you pass through the standing waves.

The halo is a topological insulator made of sound, round you: a disc of four hundred sites, two orbitals each, whose hopping turns the phase so that the inside is gapped and the rim carries waves that can run one way only (the model of Qi, Wu and Zhang, a Chern insulator). Every note of the chord has its own lattice and strikes the rim from a direction of its own; what it starts runs round you -- the same way, whatever is in the way: Disorder fills the inside with random potentials and the rim runs on (that is what topological protection is). Once Round: how long a circle takes; Way Round: which way. Topology turns the lattice from topological to trivial: then the gap holds nothing, and the sound stays where it was struck.

Time Mirror puts a ring of sixty-four listening cells round you in the wave room: they record the air for a window (Mirror Window), and during the next send it back reversed -- and the waves, run backwards, gather where they came from (the time-reversal mirror of Fink's laboratory). Every sound returns as a swell that converges on its own source, the room's reverberation played inwards. Then the mirror listens again: it never hears itself, so it never runs away.

Headset: with Empyrean's Quest app the head and the hands reach into the cloud (see Family jam and headset). Hands: how strongly -- the wind flows round them (their wake sets the chimes and the leaves going), the halo's rim goes round them, in the wave room an open hand absorbs and a pinching one becomes the voices' source, and the slime mould feeds where they are, at the pitch of their height. Head Tracking keeps the binaural cloud and the thunder where they are in the room as you turn your head.

Deep Channel (on the Rooms page): the ocean's sound channel. Deep in the sea the speed of sound is least about a kilometre down (Munk's profile), and sound there is bent back towards the axis again and again and carried across an ocean. Its rays arrive one after another -- the steep ones first, through faster water, the flat ones last and loudest --, so a sound comes back as a swell that ends at once. The rays are traced through the profile for Range, and the water darkens what it carries the farther it goes; Off Axis puts the source below the axis, for more and steeper rays and a longer swell.

The modal hall and its growth

The modal hall is a reverb made the way the instrument is made: sixty-five thousand resonances on the graphics card, each a sine that dies away, woken by everything the voices play. Decay is the time it takes to fall silent (60 dB) in the low range; Damping lets the upper octaves die sooner. Tuned puts that share of the resonances on the pitches of the scale, and Spread scatters them a few cents around their notes: with Tuned up, the hall rings in key, like the sympathetic strings of a viola d'amore, and retunes without a click when the scale or its root changes. Drift lets every resonance wander by a few cents, Width gives each its own place in each ear, Lowest keeps the bottom free. Breath lets every resonance's decay wander slowly, so the hall's colour moves while it rings. Paint lets the hall be heard through the landscape: its resonances light up where the landscape is bright, so the tail draws the aurora, the rain, the tides. Freeze holds the hall as it is, its input shut: a chord left hanging in the air.

Grow gives the hall a memory. It listens to everything that sounds, band by band over the octaves, and remembers how much it heard where, against the average -- a step of Memory towards what it hears while anything sounds (Memory: a minute to four hours). Growth (Grow above nothing) lets the bands it heard much ring longer, up to almost three times, and the bands it never heard a little shorter: the hall grows into the music played in it, and after an evening of one key it rings in that key of its own accord. Erosion (Grow below nothing) wears it away where it was used: those bands die sooner, and what was rarely played rings on. At nothing it neither learns nor changes.

The memory is the instrument's, not a preset's: it stays through every preset change, is kept with the session, and the curve below the knobs shows it, band by band. It can be painted too: drag across the curve and the memory rises where the mouse is above the line and falls where it is below -- a hall that rings long in the low octaves before a note was played, or one worn quiet in the middle. The memory is heard only while the hall grows from it: with Grow at nothing, painting sets it to 0.4. Save writes it into Documents/Empyrean/Halls, Load brings back a hall that grew elsewhere, New hall forgets everything.

Modulation

Four LFOs, two envelopes and four macros move the instrument's knobs through a matrix of sixteen rows: a source, a target, a depth. The LFOs are slow by default (their rates step by the golden ratio, so they never line up) and have no hard edges: Square and Ramp glide through their jumps in a few percent of a cycle, Random is a drifting curve, Steps glides from one value to the next. The envelopes are shapes from the motions' bank, played from the start whenever a note arrives into silence; Rate sets how fast they run.

The macros are knobs without a place of their own: they mean what the matrix says. Every factory preset gives them the same meaning, so that a controller mapped to them plays every preset: Macro 1 the motion (Depth and Rate), Macro 2 the brightness or the filter, Macro 3 the air, Macro 4 the depth in the space. The mod wheel, channel pressure, the velocity of the last note and a slow random curve are sources too.

Depth is measured in the knob's own travel: 0.5 moves a knob by half its range up and down, whatever its unit. A knob that is modulated shows a ring around it where its value is now, and every row on it as an arc outside, in its source's colour (the colours of the sources' traces on this page): the span the row can move the knob over, and a dot where it moves it now.

The rows can be made where the knob is, on any page: right-click a knob. Its menu lists the rows on it, each with its depth to drag and an x to remove it, and Modulate with offers every source, sorted by kind -- a new row in the first free one, at a fifth of the knob's range. This page shows them as they are made.

Fields and chaos are Noctuary's. Lenia is a living field -- Conway's Life made continuous -- whose blobs drift, pulse, split and die, read at four places (Lenia Rate, Lenia Growth). Lorenz and Roessler are two strange attractors, never the same path twice (Chaos Period). Kura 1-4 are four slow oscillators coupled by Coherence: free at 0, falling into step towards 1, never quite as one. Three come from the piece itself: the Beat runs at the rate the lowest interval beats -- still when it is pure, quicker the further out of tune it drifts -- Cascade swells with the conductor's excitement (Cascade on the Conductor page lets its events breed events), Root Age climbs over ten minutes since the root last moved. Note, Distance and Random per Note follow the loudest voice and the last note.

Four sources are what the instrument hears of itself: Loudness, Heard Brightness (where its energy stands), Change (how fast its spectrum moves) and Roughness (how much its chord grinds, by Sethares' measure of how its voices' harmonics rub). Route them back into the sound and it regulates itself: roughness into Self-Tune, and the rougher it gets the harder it tunes; loudness against the far level, and it recedes when it swells.

The reservoir is a small network of 2048 neurons on the graphics card, coupled at random, listening to what the instrument hears and plays (its loudness, brightness, change and roughness, the Beat, the note, the conductor's excitement, Lenia) -- each measured against its last half minute, so it hears what changes. Its four readouts, Reservoir 1-4, are smooth curves that never repeat and remember what was played. Chaos is how strongly its neurons drive each other: below 1 it forgets and follows the music, about 1 it rings on at the edge of chaos, above it runs on by itself. Memory is how slowly it moves, Input how closely it listens. It is only computed while a row of the matrix reads it.

The Modulation page has the sixteen rows, and beside them every source live, coloured by its kind: the LFOs, envelopes, macros and the keyboard's; fields and chaos; the piece; what the instrument hears; the reservoir. So you can see which one breathes slowly, which one jumps and which one listens before you route it. Click a source and the inspector shows it with its own knobs -- an LFO's shape and rate, an envelope's, a macro, Lenia's rate and growth, the attractors' period, the Kuramoto bank's coherence, the reservoir's chaos, memory and input: the modulators' knobs live there. While the window is open the reservoir runs even when no row reads it.

Every row of the matrix can have a via source: the depth is scaled by its amount, 0 to 1 -- an LFO that only moves the filter as far as the mod wheel allows, a Lorenz wander that opens with Root Age.

Three sources are the night's: Night, the real night where you are (0 while the sun is up, 1 in astronomical night: route it to a darker colour or a slower motion), Risset Pulse, the rhythm that slows for ever (Dreams page), and Lightning, a strike's flash, before its thunder.

Glide, morph and journeys

Every voice here is a spectrum written afresh every block, so one sound can become another rather than fade into it. Glide makes every preset you choose -- with the arrows, from the list, by program change or from the headset -- travel from the sound before it over that many seconds: every number on its way, the partials sliding into the new scale, the filters moving into each other -- by transport: the pass band of one travels to where the other's is, a formant wanders to its new place, rather than one fading out while the other fades in -- a motion or a living landscape fading out where it changes and in again -- with whatever the matrix adds to its depth. Each sound brings its own LFOs and envelopes: on the way, the rows of the sound being left keep running on its modulators while they fade, the new sound's rows come in on its own, so a slow breath on the brightness does not quicken because the next sound uses LFO 1 for quick steps on the width. A preset you choose is the one you hear: whatever was choosing the sound lets go -- Morph switches off, a journey or a route stops, the map lets go and its cursor goes to the preset's point. The knobs show where the sound is going; a knob turned on the way changes where it goes.

Morph holds the sound between two slots. A and B take the sound as it plays (This sound), a preset from the bank or a snapshot of the Focus page (Preset ..., the snapshots first); with Morph on, A - B moves between them, the matrix included -- a knob for a controller, the mod wheel's neighbour, a hand in the headset. Switching Morph on or off glides too.

A journey is presets in a row, each held for a while and glided into the next, round and round: a sleep concert for a whole night. The instrument comes with five hundred and thirty-one: for every group a Journey (twelve of its different faces, six to twelve minutes each, glides of one to two and a half) and a Night (its twelve stillest, ten to eighteen minutes, glides of two to four), and nineteen Whole Nights across groups (twenty-four presets, twelve to twenty minutes each, about six and a half hours before the first comes round again) -- two of them, Lullabies and Deep Sleep, from the sleep concerts, one each from the visions, the swarms and currents, the halls and orbits, the harmonic lives, the dreams and phantoms, the weather and growth, the rooms and topology, the night and illusions, the choruses and mirrors, the deep floors, the stars and gravity, the light and depth and the plasma and voice. The times are drawn anew every time, so no night runs the same. A journey keeps the conductor playing, through presets made for the hands too. Choose one and press play; the status line shows where it is. To write your own, + adds the preset playing, the disk saves it into Documents/Empyrean/Journeys; the files are plain text, one preset a line with its time and its glide, and can be edited by hand.

Under the journeys, the timeline: the journey chosen (or under way) as a band over time, each step as wide as it lasts on average, its glide a ramp into it, the preset's name on it, the hours under it; while it runs, the step under way is lit and fills as its time goes. A click on a step goes there: the journey jumps to it (and starts there when none runs).

Under it, the journey you are writing, drawn the same way and changed by hand: drag a step's right edge for how long it stays, its ramp for how long it glides in (to the left: longer), the block itself to move it in the row; right-click a step to remove it. The times keep their spread, only longer or shorter. Edit a copy makes the journey above yours to change -- the disk then saves it under its own name.

The map and its routes

The map lays every preset of the bank on a square by what it sounds like, measured: across from darker to brighter (where its energy stands), up from still to moving (how much its spectrum changes over a quarter of a second, a second and three seconds). Where many presets sound alike they crowd -- the darker, gently moving middle of the bank, where most of the sleep concerts are -- and where none sounds like that the map is thin or empty. With Map on, the sound is the blend of the presets round the cursor, and it follows the cursor over Map Glide. Radius is how far the blend reaches, counted in the spacing of the presets there: under a half, a preset's own point plays that preset and one turns into the next only near the middle between them; at 1 two neighbours share most of the way; at 4 six blend wherever the cursor stands; in an empty stretch the blend widens with the distances. The blend is made as the glides are: every number on its way, a motion or a living landscape fading out where the neighbours disagree, the filters by their share, the matrices weighted, each row with its own preset's modulators. Click a preset to go there, drag to wander between them; the presets that blend are ringed, the name of the one under the mouse is shown. The wheel zooms the map round the mouse, up to 32 times (a pinch on a touchpad too), and a drag with the right or the middle button moves the view: zoomed in, a small map in the corner shows where the view is (click or drag in it to go there), Whole map goes back, and where few enough presets are in view their names stand at their points. When the cursor -- or, with the map off, the preset playing -- leaves the view by itself (a route walking, a preset chosen), the view glides after it; while it is out of sight, a ring at the edge points to it. Choosing a preset with the arrows or the list plays that preset: the map lets go and its cursor goes to the preset's point -- click the map to take it up again. Switching the map off leaves the knobs on the preset nearest the cursor. On the map the conductor plays or rests as you set it; a route starts it.

A route walks the cursor over the map: waypoints, each a preset or a place, with the time to travel there -- how long the sound takes to turn into the next -- the time to stay, and a radius. Thirteen come with the instrument (Dawn, Dusk, Round the Map, the shores, Stillness, Weather, Spiral In, and tours of the five parts of the bank); Shift-click on the map, + Cursor or + Preset add waypoints, a waypoint dragged moves (over a preset it snaps to it), a right-click on it removes it, the times are typed in as minutes and seconds, the disk saves it into Documents/Empyrean/Routes (plain text, a waypoint a line). Route Speed walks it faster or slower, Loop round and round.
